<div id="anp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none"><div class="notes p"><section id="historyarticle-meta1" class="history"><p>Received 2023 Aug 30; Revised 2023 Sep 22; Accepted 2023 Sep 28; Collection date 2023 Oct.</p></section></div></div>
<div id="clp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none">

<p>Licensee MDPI, Basel, Switzerland. This article is an open access article distributed under the terms and conditions of the Creative Commons Attribution (CC BY) license (<a href="https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/</a>).</p>
<div class="p"><a href="/about/copyright/" class="usa-link">PMC Copyright notice</a></div>
</div>
</div>
<div>PMCID: PMC10608729  PMID: <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37887375/" class="usa-link">37887375</a>
</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p><em>Doliocarpus dentatus</em> is thought to have a wide variety of therapeutic phytochemicals that allegedly improve libido and cure impotence. Although a few biomarkers have been identified with potential antinociceptive and cytotoxic properties, an untargeted mass spectrometry-based metabolomics approach has never been undertaken to identify therapeutic biofingerprints for conditions, such as erectile dysfunction, in men. This study executes a preliminary phytochemical screening of the woody vine of two ecotypes of <em>D. dentatus</em> with renowned differences in therapeutic potential for erectile dysfunction. Liquid chromatography–mass spectrometry-based metabolomics was used to screen for flavonoids, terpenoids, and other chemical classes found to contrast between red and white ecotypes. Among the metabolite chemodiversity found in the ecotype screens, using a combination of GNPS, MS-DIAL, and SIRIUS, approximately 847 compounds were annotated at levels 2 to 4, with the majority of compounds falling under lipid and lipid-like molecules, benzenoids and phenylpropanoids, and polyketides, indicative of the contributions of the flavonoid, shikimic acid, and terpenoid biosynthesis pathways. Despite the extensive annotation, we report on 138 tentative compound identifications of potentially therapeutic compounds, with 55 selected compounds at a level-2 annotation, and 22 statistically significant therapeutic biomarkers, the majority of which were polyphenols. Epicatechin methyl gallate, catechin gallate, and proanthocyanidin A2 had the greatest significant differences and were also relatively abundant among the red and white ecotypes. These putatively identified compounds reportedly act as antioxidants, neutralizing damaging free radicals, and lowering cell oxidative stress, thus aiding in potentially preventing cellular damage and promoting overall well-being, especially for treating erectile dysfunction (ED).</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> Capadulla, erectile dysfunction, mass spectrometry, metabolomics, polyphenols, antioxidants</p></section></section><section id="sec1-metabolites-13-01050"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. Introduction</h2>
<p>Doliocarpus dentatus, popularly known as Guyanese Capadulla, is an important medical woody stem belonging to the Dilleniaceae family [<a href="#B1-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B1-metabolites-13-01050">1</a>,<a href="#B2-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2-metabolites-13-01050">2</a>]. The <em>D. dentatus</em> species is commonly found in tropical rainforests in Central and South Americas, from Mexico to South Brazil, and the Amazon region has the highest representation of the species [<a href="#B3-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3-metabolites-13-01050">3</a>]. In Guyana, the woody vine of <em>D. dentatus</em> grows in mixed to evergreen lowland forests [<a href="#B4-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4-metabolites-13-01050">4</a>,<a href="#B5-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B5-metabolites-13-01050">5</a>,<a href="#B6-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B6-metabolites-13-01050">6</a>,<a href="#B7-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-metabolites-13-01050">7</a>]. The Capadulla woody vine is regarded locally as a powerful aphrodisiac [<a href="#B8-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B8-metabolites-13-01050">8</a>,<a href="#B9-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B9-metabolites-13-01050">9</a>]. The stem of the <em>D. dentatus</em> is used to make tea or drinks, which purportedly raises libido, cures impotence, and improves sexual desire in men [<a href="#B9-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B9-metabolites-13-01050">9</a>].</p>
<p>In Guyana, the traditional use of <em>D. dentatus</em> has involved treating specific symptoms of diabetes, Leishmanial ulcers, and erectile dysfunction (ED), and supporting overall well-being [<a href="#B10-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-metabolites-13-01050">10</a>,<a href="#B11-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B11-metabolites-13-01050">11</a>]. </p>
<p>The differences between <em>D. dentatus</em> red and white ecotypes are recognized by their wood color instead of their botanical features, such as leaves and fertile organs [<a href="#B12-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B12-metabolites-13-01050">12</a>]. Two ecotypes of <em>D. dentatus</em> (i.e., white and red) are used similarly as an aphrodisiac without reported noticeable differences in their efficacy by Guyanese locals. Informants have also indicated that the <em>D. dentatus</em> red species is considered a better aphrodisiac than the Capadulla white ecotype, and that “Capadulla red is considered a male species consumed by males”. At the same time, “Capadulla white is regarded as the female species but is consumed when Capadulla red is in limited supply”. </p>
<p>In earlier studies on <em>D. dentatus</em>, using mass spectrometry, total phenolic (204.04 mg/g), flavonoid (89.17 mg/g), and tannins (12.05 mg/g) contents, and sitosterol-3-O-D-glucopyranoside, kaempferol 3-O-L-aminopyranoside, betulinic acid, and betulin were discovered in the ethanolic extract of <em>D. dentatus</em> leaves, which evaluated for widespread use against pain [<a href="#B13-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13-metabolites-13-01050">13</a>]. An additional phytochemical analysis confirmed the presence of butyric acid, steroids, lactones, anthracensides, betulinic acid, tannins, flavones, and phenolic acids in this plant [<a href="#B14-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14-metabolites-13-01050">14</a>]. </p>
<p><em>D. dentatus</em> leaves’ ethanolic extract was evaluated for widespread use against pain in animal models and its toxicogenicity [<a href="#B15-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-metabolites-13-01050">15</a>]. The results indicated that an ethnopharmacological dose of the aqueous extract from the leaves of <em>D. dentatus</em> decreased the pain response, indicating analgesic and anti-inflammatory actions. Furthermore, toxicogenic assays determined that the aqueous extract of <em>D. dentatus</em> did not have genotoxic potential and did not modify splenic phagocytosis [<a href="#B3-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3-metabolites-13-01050">3</a>,<a href="#B15-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-metabolites-13-01050">15</a>]. Another study suggested that the extract from the leaves of <em>Capadulla</em> had antibacterial and anti-inflammatory properties, demonstrating that the leaves had phytochemical compounds that could treat various illnesses [<a href="#B13-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13-metabolites-13-01050">13</a>,<a href="#B16-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B16-metabolites-13-01050">16</a>]. The extract from the leaves of <em>D. dentatus</em> is safe to use during the gestational period. It does not cause DNA damage and is not teratogenic [<a href="#B16-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B16-metabolites-13-01050">16</a>]. </p>
<p>In vitro studies of the biological activity of <em>D. dentatus</em> have shown that it is an antimicrobial against strains of <em>Escherichia coli</em>, <em>Klebsiella pneumoniae</em>, and <em>Staphylococcus aureus</em>; anti-inflammatory; and cytotoxic in leukemic cells of the K562 lineage [<a href="#B13-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13-metabolites-13-01050">13</a>]. It is effective against amastigotes of <em>Leishmania amazonenses</em> [<a href="#B17-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-metabolites-13-01050">17</a>,<a href="#B18-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-metabolites-13-01050">18</a>] and does not cause genomic or chromosomal damage, implying that it is safe for use [<a href="#B14-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14-metabolites-13-01050">14</a>,<a href="#B15-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-metabolites-13-01050">15</a>,<a href="#B16-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B16-metabolites-13-01050">16</a>,<a href="#B19-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-metabolites-13-01050">19</a>]. </p>
<p>Although many phytochemicals have been discovered in <em>D. dentatus leaves,</em> the woody vine of <em>Doliocarpus dentatus</em> has not been investigated using mass spectrometry-based metabolomics and other bioinformatics techniques. The objective of the current study is to determine the phytochemical profile of <em>D. dentatus</em> ecotypes utilizing untargeted and semi-targeted plant metabolomics using liquid chromatography (LC) coupled with electrospray ionization mass spectrometry (ESI-MS/MS). Liquid chromatography–electrospray ionization-–mass spectrometry (LC-ESI-MS) is a sensitive technology for determining compound molecular masses. ESI-tandem mass spectrometry (MS/MS) can be utilized to obtain additional structural information on compound fragmentation patterns [<a href="#B20-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B20-metabolites-13-01050">20</a>,<a href="#B21-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B21-metabolites-13-01050">21</a>]. LC-MS-based metabolomics methods are commonly used to profile complex biological extracts, such as those from plants [<a href="#B22-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B22-metabolites-13-01050">22</a>,<a href="#B23-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-metabolites-13-01050">23</a>], with non-targeted plant metabolomics being of particular interest, owing to the large plant kingdom’s metabolite variety [<a href="#B24-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B24-metabolites-13-01050">24</a>,<a href="#B25-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B25-metabolites-13-01050">25</a>]. </p>
<p>The goal of this work is to perform a preliminary investigation of the phytochemical profiles of <em>D. dentatus</em> that will provide additional information on the potential use of the compounds, compare compounds in both ecotypes, and identify any unique or beneficial compounds present in <em>D. dentatus</em> using mass spectrometry-based metabolomics. This information can be used to better understand the physiology and ecology of this species and potentially inform conservation efforts. It may also affect human health, as <em>D. dentatus</em> is a known source of bioactive compounds. Additionally, understanding the metabolic pathways of this plant can eventually help target new therapies for erectile dysfunction in men. </p></section><section id="sec2-metabolites-13-01050"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. Materials and Methods</h2>
<section id="sec2dot1-metabolites-13-01050"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1. Chemicals Used for Metabolite Extraction and Liquid Chromatography–Mass Spectrometry</h3>
<p>HPLC-grade solvents, such as acetonitrile and methanol, were purchased from Fisher Scientific (Hampton, NH, USA). Water used for metabolite extraction and the mobile phase in liquid chromatography–mass spectrometry (LC-MS) was previously purified using a Milli-Q system (Millipore, Burlington, MA, USA). Benzyladenine and abscisic acid-labeled standards were acquired from OlChemim Ltd., Olomouc, Czech Republic. </p></section><section id="sec2dot2-metabolites-13-01050"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2. Study Site and Plots–Eagle Mountain Forest Potaro–Siparuni, Guyana</h3>
<p>The Eagle Mountain Forest is about 200 km south–southwest of Georgetown, Guyana’s capital city (21N0261909-0578704). The Eagle Forest, located in an isolated area, is characterized by steep sandstone ridges ranging in height from 100–724.8 m above sea level. Annual rainfall is very high (3500–4000 mm), with noticeable peaks in May and June. The study area is ecologically significant, supporting a high diversity of flora and fauna. It is dominated by Leguminosae/Fabaceae subfamilies (Mimosaceae, Caesalpiniaceae, and Papilionaceae) [<a href="#B26-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B26-metabolites-13-01050">26</a>,<a href="#B27-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27-metabolites-13-01050">27</a>]. <em>D. dentatus</em> liana was collected randomly after an inventory of all <em>D. dentatus</em> lianas had been created and their position recorded. The measurement point for all <em>D. dentatus</em> liana was 3–5 cm representing seedlings, and poles, 20 cm and longer, representing mature individuals in the diameter range, known to capture the demography for seedling poles and mature individuals [<a href="#B28-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28-metabolites-13-01050">28</a>]. In July 2022, we collected a total of four biological samples, all of which were chosen at random from the <em>D. dentatus</em> liana population present at the Eagle Mountain study location. Collections included red and white <em>D. dentatus</em>, utilizing diameter classes (50–200 mm). </p>
<p>For every individual <em>D. dentatus</em> liana selected, a 20 mm disc was carefully extracted from various points along the woody vine, encompassing the ground level, an intermediate position, and an emergent point. The processed samples of the same ecotypes with similar diameters formed a composite sample. Following the extraction, these samples were promptly placed in dry ice to maintain their metabolic integrity during transit. Subsequently, they were transported to the University of Guyana (Greater Georgetown, Guyana). Here, the samples underwent further processing to prepare them for an in-depth analysis. Finally, the samples were sent to Trent University (Peterborough, Canada), where an advanced metabolomic analysis was conducted.</p></section><section id="sec2dot3-metabolites-13-01050"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. Biological Sample Preparation and Metabolite Extraction</h3>
<p>Metabolite extraction was performed as previously published [<a href="#B29-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29-metabolites-13-01050">29</a>]. Thirty mg of <em>D. dentatus</em> dried tissues were pre-weighed before the extraction. Tissues in 2 mL Eppendorf tubes with 2 zirconium grinding beads (Comeau Technique Ltd., Vandreuil-Dorion, QC, Canada) were loaded into a Retsch MM300 ball mill (Haan, Germany) where they were shaken for 5 minutes at 25 Hz. The powder was suspended in 1 mL of ice-cooled 80% MeOH/20% double-distilled water <em>v</em>/<em>v</em> and sonicated for 15 min. The tubes were centrifuged (Sorvall ST 16 centrifuge; ThermoScientific, Waltham, MA, USA) at 10,000 rpm for 10 min. A total of 700 μL of sample supernatant was transferred to 0.2 μM PVDF centrifuge filters with 2 mL receiver tubes and spun down at 10,000 rpm for 5 min. Filtered extracts were dried at ambient temperature in the speed vacuum centrifuge (ThermoScientific Savant SpeedVac<sup>TM</sup>, Rockford, IL, USA). Residues were redissolved in 400 μL 50% MeOH/water and spun down again at 10,000 rpm. A total of 300 μL of supernatant was transferred to a 350 μL autosampler insert inside a 2 mL glass vial with a septa cap. A total of 10 μL (10 ng) of benzyladenine and 27 μL (59 ng) of abscisic acid-labeled standards (OlChemim Ltd., Olomouc, Czech Republic) were added to each vial to monitor the retention time deviation across samples for positive and negative ionization modes, respectively.</p></section><section id="sec2dot4-metabolites-13-01050"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.4. Data Acquisition via Liquid Chromatography–Mass Spectrometry (LC-MS)</h3>
<p>Extracts from both <em>D. dentatus</em> ecotypes (i.e., red and white) were analyzed by LC-MS [<a href="#B29-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29-metabolites-13-01050">29</a>] with minor modifications. For an untargeted metabolomics analysis, the data were acquired using a Thermo Q-Exactive Orbitrap mass spectrometer (Thermo Scientific, San Jose, CA, USA) coupled to a Thermo Dionex Ultimate 3000 Liquid Chromatograph System (Thermo Scientific, San Jose, CA, USA). All samples were injected for analysis in a mixed mode (positive and negative electrospray ionization (ESI) modes) with an initial mass range of <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 80−600 at a resolution of 140,000 at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 200 full width at half minimum (FWHM), with an automatic gain control (AGC) target of 3 × 10<sup>6</sup>, and maximum injection time (IT) of 524 ms. The following conditions were used for the heated electrospray ionization (HESI) probe: capillary temperature, 250 °C; sheath gas, 30 arbitrary units; auxiliary gas, 8 units; probe heater temperature, 450 °C; S-Lens rf level, 60%; and capillary voltage, 3.9 kV.</p>
<p>Quality controls (QCs) composed of equal volumes of sample replicates, were used for data-dependent tandem mass spectrometry (ddms<sup>2</sup>). QCs were created with a mixture of all replicates from both ecotypes and also from each ecotype. Fragmentation using ddms<sup>2</sup> was performed at a resolution of 17,500 with an AGC target of 5 × 10<sup>5</sup> within a narrower mass range of 100 to 600 <em>m</em>/<em>z</em>. The fragmentation was triggered at a loop count of 10 (top 10 most intense peaks per scan), with a precursor isolation window of 1 <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> at a normalized collision energy (NCE) of 35 eV. The maximum IT was 64 ms. </p>
<p>The chromatographic separation was accomplished with an HGP-3400RS dual pump and WPS-3000 autosampler equipped with a Kinetex C<sub>18</sub> column (2.1 i.d × 50 mm, 2.6 μm particle size, Phenomenex, Torrace, CA, USA) operated at an approximate room temperature of 22 °C. The instrument control was achieved with Chromeleon Chromatography Data System software version 6.8 (ThermoScientific; Ottawa, ON, Canada). For the separation, component A comprised ddH<sub>2</sub>0 with 0.1% formic acid, and component B comprised acetonitrile (CH<sub>3</sub>CN) with 0.1% formic acid, at a flow rate of 0.3 mL/min. Mobile phase B was held at 0% for 30 s, before increasing it to 100% over 3 min. Solvent B was then held at 100% for 2 min before returning to 0% over 4 min for column re-equilibration. The total runtime was 10 min, with a sample injection volume of 25 μL. </p></section><section id="sec2dot5-metabolites-13-01050"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.5. Data Processing of Untargeted Mass Spectrometry Data </h3>
<p>The MS Convert utility (Version 3.0) of the open-source ProteoWizard program was used to convert raw mass spectrometric data into an mzXML format [<a href="#B30-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30-metabolites-13-01050">30</a>]. As the LC-MS acquisition occurred in the mixed ionization mode, negative and position ions were extracted separately by using the default for peak picking and the threshold peak filter with absolute intensity using the most intense ions (0.001 and above). Peak peaking, retention time correction, and peak grouping were all conducted using the default parameters of XCMS Online [<a href="#B31-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-metabolites-13-01050">31</a>], which is included in the <a href="#app1-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials</a>. The pairwise option in XCMS Online was used with the white ecotype as the control [<a href="#B32-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32-metabolites-13-01050">32</a>]. After peak peaking, retention time correction, and peak grouping, the resulting peak intensity matrix was subjected to a statistical analysis using MetaboAnalyst 5.0 [<a href="#B32-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32-metabolites-13-01050">32</a>]. The data were normalized by sum, and then log-transformed before a principal component analysis (PCA) to visualize the overall clustering pattern of the samples. Additionally, a partial least squares discriminant analysis (PLS-DA) was performed to identify the metabolites that contributed most to the separation between sample groups. The significance of differential metabolites was determined using the <em>t</em>-test and fold-change analysis.</p>
<p><em>D. dentatus</em> red and white putative MS<sup>1</sup> <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> features were compared to databases of physiologically active substances in the literature and with bioinformatics servers used in untargeted metabolomics, such as KnapSack [<a href="#B33-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33-metabolites-13-01050">33</a>], KEGG [<a href="#B34-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B34-metabolites-13-01050">34</a>], the Plant Metabolic Network using the PlantCyc database (<a href="https://plantcyc.org" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://plantcyc.org</a>, accessed on 10 June 2023) [<a href="#B35-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B35-metabolites-13-01050">35</a>], MetaboQuest [<a href="#B36-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36-metabolites-13-01050">36</a>], SciFinder [<a href="#B37-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B37-metabolites-13-01050">37</a>], and PubChem [<a href="#B38-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-metabolites-13-01050">38</a>]. In the processing module of XCalibur 4.1 (ThermoScientific, Waltham, MA, USA), we inputted the putative identities of key therapeutic compound families (i.e., flavonoids, terpenoids, and alkaloids). They extracted the exact masses of protonated or deprotonated compounds.</p>
<p>The putative identities of the key therapeutic compound families (i.e., flavonoids, terpenoids, and alkaloids) generated were inputted in the processing module in XCalibur 4.1 (ThermoScientific, Waltham, MA, USA) where the exact masses of protonated or deprotonated compounds were extracted. This semi-targeted approach was performed to determine peak quality and to monitor the peaks within the samples for therapeutic compounds falling under polyphenolics, terpenoids, and alkaloids. The relative quantification of peak areas was performed to determine up-or-downregulated compounds, by the ratio of the averages of [Capadulla Red]/[Capadulla White]. If the ratio (fold change; FC) &gt; 1, the samples were upregulated. The converse was downregulated if the ratio &lt; 1 <a href="#app1-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link">(Supplemental Figure S2)</a>. </p>
<p>Metabolite Autoplotter was used to generate images for tentative metabolites with corresponding peak areas [<a href="#B39-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39-metabolites-13-01050">39</a>] The workflow for data processing can be seen in <a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a> for MS<sup>1</sup> data.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="metabolites-13-01050-f001"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10608729_metabolites-13-01050-g001.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/6cb5/10608729/fc4acd74f78e/metabolites-13-01050-g001.jpg" loading="lazy" height="401" width="773" alt="Figure 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/metabolites-13-01050-f001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Metabolomics workflow used for extracting, acquiring, and analyzing compounds present in <em>D. dentatus</em> ecotypes. Icons used are sourced from The Noun Project.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec2dot6-metabolites-13-01050"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.6. Statistical Analysis of Metabolomics Datasets</h3>
<p>Untargeted metabolomic analyses generate complex multivariate datasets with thousands of tentative metabolite features and their corresponding intensities. Various dimensionality reduction procedures are employed to simplify the data and understand the relationships among the samples based on these intensities. Metabolomic data analysis is characterized by high dimensionality, where the number of measured metabolite features exceeds the number of samples and collinearity among the features. These factors challenge traditional linear regression methods, necessitating statistical procedures capable of handling collinearity [<a href="#B40-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40-metabolites-13-01050">40</a>].</p>
<p>Before any statistical analysis, preprocessing steps were applied to normalize the data to facilitate further analyses. This involved sum normalization, log<sub>2</sub> transformation, and Pareto scaling to ensure the appropriate scaling of the obtained data using the MetaboAnalyst 5.0 platform [<a href="#B41-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B41-metabolites-13-01050">41</a>] (<a href="http://www.metaboanalyst.ca" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">www.metaboanalyst.ca</a>, accessed on 10 June 2023). By normalizing the data through sum normalization, applying a log<sub>2</sub> transformation, and performing Pareto scaling, the dataset was prepared for a subsequent statistical analysis. These preprocessing steps helped to ensure that the data were appropriately scaled and standardized, allowing for meaningful comparisons and accurate statistical inference. We used ANOVA and the volcano plot to find metabolites strongly affected by the experimental factor, which was the difference between the red and white ecotypes.</p>
<p>The XCMS Online software tool was utilized to annotate the LC-MS dataset, which was subsequently analyzed through the MetaboAnalyst 5.0 platform. The methodology was employed to identify the differential metabolites involved using the standard volcano plot, which integrated the <em>t</em>-test and fold-change techniques. The graph displays the logarithmic transformation of the fold-change values on the X-axis and the negative logarithmic transformation of the <em>p</em>-values obtained from the <em>t</em>-test on the Y-axis [<a href="#B42-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42-metabolites-13-01050">42</a>].</p>
<p>Furthermore, this analysis seeks to uncover potential <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> biofingerprints that are elevated or downregulated in <em>D. dentatus</em> red and white ecotypes that may have therapeutic potential for erectile dysfunction. These statistical approaches, combined with the visual representation provided by the volcano plot, principal component analysis (PCA), partial least-squares discriminant analysis (PLS-DA), and hierarchical cluster analysis (HCA), offer valuable insights into the metabolomic variations between the <em>D. dentatus</em> red and white ecotypes. </p>
<p>Principal component analysis and partial least-squares discriminant analysis are commonly employed to reduce the dimensionality of the data and identify differences between the groups. PCA aims to replace correlated variables with uncorrelated variables, known as principal components (PCs), which capture most of the variability in the original dataset. This allows for initial biological conclusions about the samples, which can be further verified using PLS-DA or orthogonal projections to latent structure DAs (OPLS-DAs). Despite having fewer samples than variables (metabolite features and intensities), multivariate statistical methods, such as PCA [<a href="#B43-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43-metabolites-13-01050">43</a>,<a href="#B44-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44-metabolites-13-01050">44</a>], PLS-DA [<a href="#B45-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45-metabolites-13-01050">45</a>], and HCA [<a href="#B46-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B46-metabolites-13-01050">46</a>], are appropriate for analyzing metabolomics datasets [<a href="#B47-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B47-metabolites-13-01050">47</a>,<a href="#B48-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B48-metabolites-13-01050">48</a>,<a href="#B49-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-metabolites-13-01050">49</a>,<a href="#B50-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-metabolites-13-01050">50</a>,<a href="#B51-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B51-metabolites-13-01050">51</a>,<a href="#B52-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B52-metabolites-13-01050">52</a>,<a href="#B53-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B53-metabolites-13-01050">53</a>].</p>
<p>In our study, we performed a PCA to examine the relationships among samples based on the relative intensities of all tentative metabolite features. PCA groups formed into clusters based on the overall similarity or metabolite profile differences. Samples with similar profiles were grouped together on the PCA plot. PCA determines these relationships solely based on the metabolite features without considering sample descriptions, such as species or treatment. Following determining the sample relationships through PCA, we identified the top 25 significantly different features (<em>p</em> ≤ 0.05) using PLS-DA or OPLS-DA. These features allowed for separating the samples into distinct groups, indicating significant differences in their accumulation behavior among ecotypes and treatments. </p>
<p>Additionally, HCA was conducted to identify the top 25 statistically significant tentative metabolite features between the ecotypes in both positive and negative modes. Pearson’s correlation was used to assess the relatedness of samples based on these 25 features (Figures 6 and 7). The results of the HCA are visualized as a heatmap, with the samples and features displayed on the X- and Y-axes, respectively, and relative metabolite intensities indicated by a color scale. The relationship among the samples is depicted using a color-coded dendrogram atop the HCA plot. Similar to PLS-DA, HCA identifies metabolite features that separate the samples into distinct clusters aligned with sample descriptions. Ward’s clustering algorithm and Pearson’s correlation were employed to construct the dendrograms, with the Euclidean distance indicated on the X-axis. All statistical analyses were performed using the MetaboAnalyst 5.0 platform [<a href="#B41-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B41-metabolites-13-01050">41</a>] (Pang, Chong et al., 2021).</p></section><section id="sec2dot7-metabolites-13-01050"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.7. Tandem Mass Spectrometry-Data Analysis for Metabolite Annotation </h3>
<p>Using diverse bioinformatic tools can aid in metabolite annotation. Global Nature Production Social Molecular Networking (GNPS), SIRIUS, and MS-DIAL were therefore used to query the fragments for putative identification [<a href="#B3-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3-metabolites-13-01050">3</a>,<a href="#B54-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54-metabolites-13-01050">54</a>]. The metabolite annotation levels using these tools were level 2 for MS-DIAL, levels 2 and 3 for GNPS, and level 3 using the in silico fragmentation tool SIRIUS, and were assigned these levels as recommended by the Metabolomics Standards Initiative [<a href="#B55-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B55-metabolites-13-01050">55</a>]. The workflow for the data analysis can be seen in <a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="metabolites-13-01050-f002"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 2.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10608729_metabolites-13-01050-g002.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/6cb5/10608729/e41296c5220c/metabolites-13-01050-g002.jpg" loading="lazy" height="392" width="746" alt="Figure 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/metabolites-13-01050-f002/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Processing of tandem mass spectrometry data using a combination of bioinformatics tools for level 2–3 annotations.</p></figcaption></figure><section id="sec2dot7dot1-metabolites-13-01050"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.7.1. Metabolite Annotation Using MS-DIAL </h4>
<p>Tandem mass spectrometry raw data were processed with MS-DIAL 5.1 [<a href="#B56-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-metabolites-13-01050">56</a>]. Automatic feature detection was performed between retention times of 0 and 10 min for mass signal extractions in positive and negative ionization modes. MS<sup>1</sup> and MS<sup>2</sup> tolerance values were set to 0.01 and 0.025 Da, respectively, in the profile mode. Minimum feature height, mass slice width, and the sigma window value were all set to the default of 1000 (Aus: arbitrary units), 0.1 Da, and 0.5, respectively [<a href="#B57-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B57-metabolites-13-01050">57</a>]. Alignments for samples were performed at a 0.015 Da mass tolerance and 0.05 min for the retention time tolerance for MS<sup>1</sup>. Database matches were performed with the parameters of MS<sup>1</sup> and MS<sup>2</sup> at 0.01 and 0.05 Da, respectively (instructions as previously published [<a href="#B58-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B58-metabolites-13-01050">58</a>]. Databases for both positive and negative ionization modes were downloaded and chosen for level 2 from the MS-DIAL website (&lt;<a href="http://prime.psc.riken.jp/compms/msdial/main.html" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://prime.psc.riken.jp/compms/msdial/main.html</a>&gt; accessed on 1 March 2023).</p></section><section id="sec2dot7dot2-metabolites-13-01050"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.7.2. Metabolite Annotation Using GNPS via Classical Molecular Networking </h4>
<p>Raw data from the quality control samples from each ecotype were converted to mzXML via MSConvert option in Proteowizard and submitted to GNPS. A molecular network for both negative and positive ionization data was created using the online workflow (<a href="https://ccms-ucsd.github.io/GNPSDocumentation/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://ccms-ucsd.github.io/GNPSDocumentation/</a>, accessed on 14 September 2023) on the GNPS website (<a href="http://gnps.ucsd.edu" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://gnps.ucsd.edu</a>, accessed on 14 September 2023). The data were filtered by removing all MS/MS fragment ions within +/− 17 Da of the precursor <em>m</em>/<em>z</em>. MS/MS spectra were window filtered by choosing only the top 6 fragment ions in the +/− 50 Da window throughout the spectrum. The precursor ion mass tolerance was set to 0.02 Da and a MS/MS fragment ion tolerance of 0.02 Da was adopted. A network was then created where the edges were filtered to have a cosine score above 0.6 and more than 3 matched peaks. Furthermore, edges between the two nodes were kept in the network if and only if each of the nodes appeared in each other’s respective top 10 most similar nodes. Finally, the maximum size of a molecular family was set to 100, and the lowest scoring edges were removed from molecular families until the molecular family size was below this threshold. The spectra in the network were then searched against GNPS’s spectral libraries. The library spectra were filtered in the same manner as the input data. All matches kept between the network and library spectra were required to have a score above 0.6 and at least 3 matched peaks [<a href="#B59-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B59-metabolites-13-01050">59</a>].</p>
<p>The workflow and results of classical molecular networking can be found in the following GNPS repositories for positive and negative ionization data (Task IDs = e06a20c58fa04355ba3630912077b3ea and 8ceaa322aac44c62a027d131b33dc4ce).</p>
<p>Dereplicator+ [<a href="#B60-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B60-metabolites-13-01050">60</a>] and Network Annotation Propagation (NAP) [<a href="#B61-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B61-metabolites-13-01050">61</a>] were used in conjunction for enhanced annotations. Although MolNetEnhancer [<a href="#B62-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B62-metabolites-13-01050">62</a>] was a workflow used for combining the results of CMN, Dereplicator+, and NAP, this function did not work for annotating our results for the visualization of compound classes via Cytoscape [<a href="#B63-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B63-metabolites-13-01050">63</a>]. However, overall visualization was performed in Cytoscape 3.10.0 after merging the networks of both negative and positive ionization modes [<a href="#B64-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B64-metabolites-13-01050">64</a>] (included in <a href="#app1-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials</a>). </p>
<p>Workflows for Dereplicator+ and NAP can be found at (Task ID for NAP: 62fec77af3ac485aa4ae473dec025e3e for the positive ionization mode and 7e718792512d4acbaeafe17952ae4f28 for the negative ionization mode. For Dereplicator+, the task IDs were 5ed74a5b3b3e47eb904ec329b9fc73cd for the positive ionization mode and 95247d7f7dbf4297963f7f451ac88ff3 for the negative ionization mode. For the merged polarity network, the task ID was 8f393dfcc9fc492c88d6d8cc6979d1c4.</p></section><section id="sec2dot7dot3-metabolites-13-01050"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.7.3. Metabolite Annotation Using GNPS via Feature-Based Molecular Networking</h4>
<p>The mzMine 2.5.3 analysis pipeline [<a href="#B65-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65-metabolites-13-01050">65</a>,<a href="#B66-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B66-metabolites-13-01050">66</a>] was used to look for MS<sup>1</sup> features corresponding to MS<sup>2</sup> fragments. The data acquired from the QC samples from each ecotype analyzed using ddMS<sup>2</sup> were converted to the mzXML format using the MSConvert option in Proteowizard, as mentioned previously. The peaks were aligned using the previously published parameters with modifications involving mass detection, chromatogram building, smoothing deconvolution, deisotoping, alignment, and gap filling [<a href="#B67-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B67-metabolites-13-01050">67</a>]. All the data (for MS<sup>1</sup> and MS<sup>2</sup>) were used and stored in.mgf format with the corresponding.csv file. </p>
<p>A molecular network was created with the Feature-Based Molecular Networking (FBMN) workflow [<a href="#B68-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B68-metabolites-13-01050">68</a>] on GNPS (<a href="https://gnps.ucsd.edu" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://gnps.ucsd.edu</a>, accessed on 14 September 2023) [<a href="#B59-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B59-metabolites-13-01050">59</a>]. The results from mzMine 2.5.3 were exported to GNPS for the FBMN analysis. FBMN was used to resolve any isomers and also to check for any other annotations due to cleaning up the data during mzMine preprocessing. The data were filtered by removing all MS/MS fragment ions within +/− 17 Da of the precursor <em>m</em>/<em>z</em>. MS/MS spectra were window filtered by choosing only the top 6 fragment ions in the +/− 50 Da window throughout the spectrum. The precursor ion mass tolerance was set to 0.02 Da and the MS/MS fragment ion tolerance to 0.02 Da. A molecular network was then created where the edges were filtered to have a cosine score above 0.6 and more than 3 matched peaks. Furthermore, the edges between two nodes were kept in the network if and only if each of the nodes appeared in each other’s respective top 10 most similar nodes. Finally, the maximum size of a molecular family was set to 100, and the lowest scoring edges were removed from molecular families until the molecular family size was below this threshold. The spectra in the network were then searched against GNPS spectral libraries [<a href="#B59-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B59-metabolites-13-01050">59</a>,<a href="#B69-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B69-metabolites-13-01050">69</a>]. The library spectra were filtered in the same manner as the input data. All matches kept between network and library spectra were required to have a score above 0.6 and at least 3 matched peaks. The DEREPLICATOR was used to annotate the MS/MS spectra [<a href="#B60-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B60-metabolites-13-01050">60</a>]. The molecular networks were visualized using Cytoscape software [<a href="#B63-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B63-metabolites-13-01050">63</a>].</p>
<p>The workflow and results of FBMN networking can be found in the following GNPS repositories for positive and negative ionization data (Task IDs = 39963758986b4f09b0b992d130489534 and 031003b2e66744fab8923d6ee405a5e2). Dereplicator+ and network annotation propagation were used in conjunction for enhanced annotations. Although MolNetEnhancer is a workflow used for combining the results of CMN, Dereplicator+ and, NAP, this function did not work FBMN for annotating our results for the visualization of compound classes via Cytoscape at that time, due to server migration. Dereplicator+ also did not work for the FBMN annotation, revealing no hits. </p>
<p>Workflows for Dereplicator+ and NAP can be found at (Task ID for NAP: 7a1f0ee11ab947408bcdf95f03a82e1b for the positive ionization mode and 12f645538264496c8a125a259f0ae810 for the negative ionization mode. For Dereplicator+, the task IDs were eef89c9cf95040628f6fb517ee64ccf7 for the positive ionization mode and eabb1d2b62124730ab10f3467310d975 for the negative ionization mode.</p>
<p>The task ID for the merged polarity network was 213e9cbd1ffb4735920662c63c96933.</p>
<p>The workflow for FBMN processing can be seen in the <a href="#app1-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials (Figure S1A)</a>. </p></section><section id="sec2dot7dot4-metabolites-13-01050"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.7.4. Using SIRIUS as an In Silico Fragmentation Tool for Metabolite Annotation</h4>
<p>mzXML files corresponding to the quality controls for each ecotype and both positive ionization modes were imported into SIRIUS 5.6.2. The peaks were classified and their MS<sup>2</sup> fragmentation patterns tentatively annotated with the ZODIAC, CSI: FingerID, CANOPUS using ClassyFire and NPClassifier modules within SIRIUS 5.6.2 [<a href="#B68-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B68-metabolites-13-01050">68</a>,<a href="#B70-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B70-metabolites-13-01050">70</a>,<a href="#B71-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B71-metabolites-13-01050">71</a>,<a href="#B72-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B72-metabolites-13-01050">72</a>,<a href="#B73-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B73-metabolites-13-01050">73</a>,<a href="#B74-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B74-metabolites-13-01050">74</a>]. Default settings were used for the different modules, with Orbitrap being the instrument of choice, and MS<sup>2</sup> queried within a 10 ppm error. For the positive ion mode, [M+H]<sup>+</sup> and [M+H-H<sub>2</sub>O]<sup>+</sup> adducts were used, with [M-H]<sup>−</sup> being the adduct used in the negative ionization mode. All databases were used for tentative identifications. For the tentative identification, a combination of COSMIC and ZODIAC scores were both considered. A manual analysis of the matching substructures was conducted before a structural assignment was performed. If the fragmentation pattern did not match the structures proposed by CSI:FingerID, the matching of fragments from the proposed structures were considered when the class was assigned using CANOPUS. If the ZODIAC score was &lt; 50%, no tentative identification was made. If the SIRIUS score was &lt; 50% with no accompanying ZODIAC score, no identification was made. A combination of scores of &gt;90% for SIRIUS and &gt;70% for ZODIAC was used for screening [<a href="#B75-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B75-metabolites-13-01050">75</a>,<a href="#B76-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B76-metabolites-13-01050">76</a>,<a href="#B77-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B77-metabolites-13-01050">77</a>]. </p></section><section id="sec2dot7dot5-metabolites-13-01050"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.7.5. Cheminformatics Using ClassyFire for Compound Class Groupings </h4>
<p>For querying the data in cleaning up tentative metabolite names, and to identify compounds not named from both GNPS and SIRIUS, the Pubchem Identifier service (<a href="https://pubchem.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/idexchange/idexchange.cgi" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://pubchem.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/idexchange/idexchange.cgi</a> accessed on 18 September 2023) was used to convert SMILES to InChiKeys for the batch compound classification tool in ClassyFire [<a href="#B73-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B73-metabolites-13-01050">73</a>]. ClassyFire Batch Compound Classification (<a href="https://cfb.fiehnlab.ucdavis.edu/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://cfb.fiehnlab.ucdavis.edu/</a> accessed on 18 September 2023) was used to categorize InChiKeys into compound classes. SMILES without corresponding InChiKeys and InChiKeys not found in ClassyFire were not used for the metabolite annotations. Additionally, duplicate data were deleted after checking InChiKeys. InChiKeys are used for structural information instead of the SMILES format as they are standardized and have a fixed length, allowing for easy data processing [<a href="#B78-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B78-metabolites-13-01050">78</a>].</p></section></section></section><section id="sec3-metabolites-13-01050"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">3. Results</h2>
<section id="sec3dot1-metabolites-13-01050"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1. Untargeted Metabolomics: Analysis of D. dentatus Red and White Ecotypes </h3>
<p>Metabolomics analyses of the woody vine <em>D. dentatus</em> red and white ecotypes in natural communities has not been previously undertaken. Therefore, in this study, we aimed to explore and describe the metabolomic differences between these ecotypes on a global level, specifically focusing on the occurrence of secondary metabolites, such as polyphenols (i.e., flavonoids), terpenoids, alkaloids, and other compounds, in the <em>D. dentatus</em> red ecotype compared to the <em>D. dentatus</em> white ecotype to identify potential biofingerprints and/or differentially expressed compounds that may have been a potential therapy for erectile dysfunction in men. This study represents one of the initial efforts to investigate and present the descriptive metabolomic results for these two ecotypes. </p>
<p>To investigate the dissimilarities in the metabolome between the <em>D. dentatus</em> red and white woody vine ecotypes, we conducted an untargeted metabolomics analysis using LC-MS/MS (liquid chromatography–tandem mass spectrometry). This allowed for the detection of 9562 tentative metabolite features that were further queried for exact and approximate matches in the databases of physiologically active substances in the literature and with bioinformatics servers used in untargeted metabolomics. </p>
<p>Among these detected features, we observed 2664 tentative metabolite features with positive ionizations [M+H]<sup>+</sup> and 2470 with negative ionizations [M-H]<sup>−</sup> in the <em>D. dentatus</em> red ecotype. Similarly, in the <em>D. dentatus</em> white ecotype, we identified 2316 tentative metabolite features with positive ionizations [M+H]<sup>+</sup>. These findings indicate variations in the metabolite profiles between the red and white ecotypes of <em>D. dentatus</em>, as demonstrated by the differing numbers of detected tentative metabolite features in each ecotype.</p>
<p>We utilized the “Functional Analysis” module and the Gene Set Enrichment Assay (GSEA) tool within MetaboAnalyst 5.0 to better understand the metabolic differences between the two ecotypes. The purpose was to compare the tentative metabolite features (9562) obtained from our analysis. Using the GSEA tool, we searched for metabolite features in the annotated <em>A. thaliana</em> metabolite database that closely matched our mass charge ratio (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em>) features. This allowed us to find similarities with the Kyoto Encyclopedia of Genes and Genomes (KEGG) database and other bioinformatics servers used in untargeted metabolomics.</p>
<p>Upon conducting the global metabolomic analysis, we found a total of 637 putative metabolite features that matched with the reference database. Among these, 107 features (16.79%) were exclusive to the <em>D. dentatus</em> red ecotype, while 64 features belonged to the <em>D. dentatus</em> white ecotype (10.05%). Notably, approximately a portion % of these putative metabolite features, about (36.58%), were unique to the <em>D. dentatus</em> red or white ecotypes (<a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>) [<a href="#B79-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B79-metabolites-13-01050">79</a>]. </p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="metabolites-13-01050-f003"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 3.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10608729_metabolites-13-01050-g003.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/6cb5/10608729/06853aa848be/metabolites-13-01050-g003.jpg" loading="lazy" height="276" width="768" alt="Figure 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/metabolites-13-01050-f003/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Venn diagrams illustrating the comparisons of tentative metabolite features and tentatively attributed pathways between the <em>D. dentatus</em> red and white ecotypes. Venn diagram (<strong>A</strong>) consists of the common and unique tentative features grouped in <em>D. dentatus</em> red- and white-ecotype woody vines. Venn diagram (<strong>B</strong>) consists of the tentatively attributed metabolite features in each woody vine <em>D. dentatus</em> ecotype using features from both ionization modes.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Utilizing the abovementioned software tools and approaches, we successfully compared numerous metabolite features and identified the putative compounds that differed between the two ecotypes at levels 3 and 4 <a href="#app1-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link">(Supplemental Tables S1–S7)</a> [<a href="#B80-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B80-metabolites-13-01050">80</a>]. This comprehensive analysis provided valuable insights into the metabolic variations within these ecotypes, further enhancing our understanding of their distinct characteristics. For further validation, MS<sup>2</sup> data processing revealed 55 selected compounds composed of flavonoids, terpenoids, and some alkaloids at a level-2 annotation level (<a href="#metabolites-13-01050-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a> and <a href="#metabolites-13-01050-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>). </p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/metabolites-13-01050-t002/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p>We utilized volcano plots to determine the tentative features in <em>D. dentatus</em> red that exhibited up- or downregulations compared to the <em>D. dentatus</em> white control in both positive and negative ionization modes. Several tentative <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> features deviated from the typical pattern within the volcano plot, indicating significant fold changes in regulating <em>D. dentatus</em> red and white ecotypes (<a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>). To validate these tentative <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> features, we referred to the Omics Craft database module MetaboQuest (<a href="http://tools.omicscraft.com/MetaboQuest/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://tools.omicscraft.com/MetaboQuest/</a>, accessed on 10 June 2023) [<a href="#B79-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B79-metabolites-13-01050">79</a>,<a href="#B80-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B80-metabolites-13-01050">80</a>]. The analysis revealed compounds, such as Methocarbamol di-TMS derivative, Diosprin, Deoxycytosine, and others (<a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>A). However, these putative compounds did not align with the biofingerprints of interest, particularly the polyphenolic compounds, such as catechin groups (<a href="#metabolites-13-01050-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a> and <a href="#metabolites-13-01050-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>). The presence of polyphenols is noteworthy as they possess antioxidant, anticancer, anti-inflammatory, cytotoxic, and antimicrobial properties, primarily due to their radical scavenging ability [<a href="#B81-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B81-metabolites-13-01050">81</a>]. These findings suggest that a further exploration of polyphenolic compounds in <em>D. dentatus</em> red and white ecotypes may hold potential as a therapeutic response to erectile dysfunction. Their beneficial effects, attributed to their diverse bioactivities, make them a promising area of interest for potential therapeutic interventions.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="metabolites-13-01050-f004"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 4.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10608729_metabolites-13-01050-g004.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/6cb5/10608729/b5e5ada1a1d3/metabolites-13-01050-g004.jpg" loading="lazy" height="659" width="771" alt="Figure 4"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/metabolites-13-01050-f004/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>(<strong>A</strong>) Volcano plot from the mass spectrometry data demonstrates the magnitude and significance of <em>D. dentatus</em> red compared with the control (<em>D. dentatus</em> white) in (<strong>A</strong>) the positive ionization mode and (<strong>B</strong>) negative ionization mode. The horizontal dashed line shows where the <em>p</em>-value is 0.05 [−log<sub>10</sub> (0.01) = 1.5], and the vertical lines show where the fold change is 2 [log<sub>2</sub> (2) = 1] or 0.5 [log<sub>2</sub> (0.5) = −1]. The two-fold change and <em>p</em>-value of 0.05 were used as the threshold cutoff. A total of 208 significantly upregulated, 166 downregulated, and 170 nonsignificant features were identified. (<strong>C</strong>) Venn diagram showing the repartition of the features obtained as statistical differences between <em>D. dentatus</em> red and white ecotypes combining both ionization modes. [<a href="#B79-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B79-metabolites-13-01050">79</a>].</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec3dot2-metabolites-13-01050"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2. Tentative Identification of the Significant Metabolites </h3>
<p>To compare the shared and unique tentative metabolite features among ecotypes, we utilized a Venn diagram [<a href="#B82-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B82-metabolites-13-01050">82</a>]. For predicting the pathways to which the tentatively identified metabolite features belonged, we compared their accurate masses against the annotated metabolite database of <em>Arabidopsis thaliana</em> using the “Functional Analysis” module and the GSEA tool within MetaboAnalyst 5.0 (<a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>). The GSEA tool searched for similar metabolite features in the annotated <em>A. thaliana</em> metabolite database and provided a ranked list of statistically significant metabolic pathways enriched in these metabolites. The list included corresponding <em>p</em>-values and adjusted <em>p</em>-values, indicating the significance level. GSEA employed a cutoff-free approach, searching for similarities between the metabolite features of the test species and <em>A. thaliana</em>. The resulting list ranked the metabolic pathways based on the similarity of metabolites and provided <em>p</em>-values derived from Kolmogorov–Smirnov tests [<a href="#B32-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32-metabolites-13-01050">32</a>,<a href="#B83-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B83-metabolites-13-01050">83</a>]. </p>
<p>The functional analysis tentatively attributed the characteristics of metabolites to 123 metabolic pathways within these two ecotypes. Out of these, 45 (95.94%) were present in both <em>D. dentatus</em> red and white ecotypes, whereas 19 (23.37%) were exclusive to the <em>D. dentatus</em> red ecotypes, and 14 (17.22%) were complete to the <em>D. dentatus</em> white ecotypes (<a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>B).</p></section><section id="sec3dot3-metabolites-13-01050"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.3. Pathway Analysis of D. dentatus Ecotypes</h3>
<p>A pathway analysis was performed to investigate the possible biomarkers within two separate ecotypes. This work aimed to elucidate the metabolic regulatory mechanisms that impacted the synthesis of diverse secondary metabolites and metabolic processes. A thorough analysis of the metabolic pathways was performed to identify the difference between the red and white ecotypes of <em>D. dentatus.</em> Databases, such as KEGG and HMDB, were used to analyze at the obtained biomarkers.</p>
<p>The research utilized a range of techniques for visualizing and analyzing the data. The data were shown using a scatter plot, and an enrichment analysis was performed using Fisher’s exact test. Furthermore, a topological study was conducted to examine the out-degree centrality. These methods were selected based on their statistical significance and capacity to evaluate the influence of routes.</p>
<p>The red ecotype of <em>D. dentatus</em> showed a statistical significance (<em>p</em> ≤ 0.05) in the pathways related to the formation of sesquiterpenoids and triterpenoids, galactose metabolism, flavonoids, steroids, flavones, and flavonols. Additional data further substantiated these findings, demonstrating statistical significance values ranging from <em>p</em> = (0.000 to 0.01586) for the pathways mentioned above. The red ecotype of <em>D. dentatus</em> was statistically involved in the biosynthesis of sesquiterpenoid and triterpenoid, the biosynthesis of flavonoid, and galactose metabolism. The significance levels for these pathways ranged from <em>p</em> = (0.03 to 0.58). In contrast, the influence of limonene and pinene degradation, as well as the production of anthocyanin, an isoquinoline alkaloid, tropane, piperidine, and pyridine alkaloid, was shown to be relatively insignificant, as illustrated in <a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>A and <a href="#app1-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link">Supplemental Table S9</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="metabolites-13-01050-f005"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 5.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10608729_metabolites-13-01050-g005.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/6cb5/10608729/a885520a1c17/metabolites-13-01050-g005.jpg" loading="lazy" height="799" width="738" alt="Figure 5"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/metabolites-13-01050-f005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>(<strong>A</strong>) Overview of pathway analysis from all metabolic pathways (KEGG and HMDB) for <em>D. dentatus</em> red and white ecotypes. All the matched pathways were classified by <em>p</em>-values (y-axis) from the pathway enrichment analysis and pathway impact values (x-axis) from the pathway topology analysis. The node size exhibits the effect of impact values. The node colors exhibit different <em>p</em>-values. (<strong>B</strong>) Integrated pathway activity profile of significant (<em>p</em> ≤ 0.05) metabolite features using both <em>D. dentatus</em> ecotypes linked to biosynthetic pathways as analyzed by MetaboAnalyst 5.0 (GSEA algorithm) using default parameters and the <em>Arabidopsis thaliana</em> pathway as the library. The color gradient from yellow to red and size of dot indicate statistical significance and impact of pathways.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Regarding the <em>D. dentatus</em> white ecotype, the metabolic pathways that exhibited the most importance were flavonoid biosynthesis, valine, leucine, and isoleucine biosynthesis. The statistical significance of these routes ranged from <em>p</em> = (0.0000 to 0.00046416). Additionally, the impact of these pathways varied, with significance levels ranging from <em>p</em> = 0.08 to 0.36. In contrast, the degradation of valine, leucine, and isoleucine; inositol phosphate metabolism; and tryptophan metabolism had reduced impacts, as depicted in <a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>B and <a href="#app1-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S8</a>. Given the previous literature on the <em>D. dentatus</em> species, it is plausible that these metabolic pathways exert substantial effects and can influence the taxonomic classification at distinct hierarchical levels [<a href="#B84-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B84-metabolites-13-01050">84</a>]. </p>
<p>In the context of biochemistry and nutrition, the interactions between compounds often yield various health benefits. These interactions are multifaceted and can result in enhanced outcomes. For example, antioxidants, such as epicatechin methyl gallate, catechin gallate, and proanthocyanidin A2, can collaborate to neutralize harmful free radicals more effectively, extending protection against oxidative damage [<a href="#B85-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B85-metabolites-13-01050">85</a>]. Additionally, some compounds can improve the absorption and utilization of others, increasing the availability of essential nutrients, such as vitamins and minerals. Metabolic interactions can also occur, enhancing the effectiveness or duration of specific compounds [<a href="#B86-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B86-metabolites-13-01050">86</a>]. Furthermore, compounds interacting with the same cellular pathways can regulate cellular processes cooperatively, leading to improved cell function and the modulation of inflammation. Nutrient synergy is another aspect where certain compounds are more effective when consumed together, while balancing effects can mitigate adverse outcomes of specific compounds when consumed alone [<a href="#B87-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B87-metabolites-13-01050">87</a>].</p>
<p>These interactions are intricate and context dependent, influenced by factors, such as the types of compounds involved, their concentrations, individual genetics, and overall dietary and lifestyle choices. Researchers continually explore these complex dynamics to better comprehend how compounds collectively impact health and well-being, especially in the case of ED.</p></section><section id="sec3dot4-metabolites-13-01050"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.4. Principal Component Analyses of D. dentatus Red and White Ecotypes</h3>
<p>Principal component analysis was conducted on all the samples based on the LC-MS data. The overall relationship among the samples was assessed by PCA using all the tentative metabolite features identified for all the samples; on a PCA plot, samples that were spatially close to each other had more similar metabolite profiles. The PCA generated two distinct clusters, each cluster corresponding to an ecotype of <em>D. dentatus</em>. The first two principal components, PC1 and PC2, explained 60.3% and 13.9%, respectively, of the variability in the positive ionization mode. In contrast, 67% and 11% in the negative ionization mode of the total variability among the samples and the clusters that corresponded to the <em>D.dentatus</em> white ecotype and <em>D. dentatus</em> red ecotype, respectively, were closer to each other, indicating overall similarities in their metabolite profiles (<a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a> and <a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f007" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>). The same pattern of relatedness was also observed in a dendrogram based on the Euclidean distance, on which the <em>D. dentatus</em> red and <em>D. dentatus</em> white ecotypes were grouped in the same clade (<a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a> and <a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f007" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="metabolites-13-01050-f006"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 6.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10608729_metabolites-13-01050-g006.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/6cb5/10608729/15e324d891bc/metabolites-13-01050-g006.jpg" loading="lazy" height="500" width="782" alt="Figure 6"></a></p>

Principal components 1 and 2 explain 60.2% and 13.9% of the variance, respectively. (<strong>B</strong>) Loading plot describing the influence of variables on sample segregation. <figcaption><p>(<strong>A</strong>) Principal component analysis score plot reflecting visualization of the relationship among the samples in terms of groupings, trends, or outliers, and showing differences between <em>D. dentatus</em> red and white ecotypes along the x−axis (PC1) and y−axis (PC2) from negative ionization data. Principal components 1 and 2 explain 66.2% and 11.2% of the variance, respectively. (<strong>B</strong>) Loading plot describing the influence of variables on sample segregation. (<strong>C</strong>) Partial least-squares discriminant analysis (PLS-DA) was applied to differentiate between <em>D. dentatus</em> red and white ecotypes. (<strong>D</strong>) Hierarchical cluster analysis (HCA), computed based on the top 25 statistically significantly different metabolite features, grouped the samples into two groups and by intensities. The accumulation of the top 15 statistically significant metabolite features that separated the samples into the two main PLS-DA clusters was mainly influenced by the relative intensity of the sample and the ecotypes (<a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a> and <a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f007" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>; <a href="#app1-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link">Supplemental Figure S1B</a>). However, a close inspection of the relative abundance of some of the top statistically significant metabolite features indicated that species and treatment influenced their abundance (<a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a> and <a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f007" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>). For instance, the relative abundance of the flavonoids was significantly higher in the <em>D.dentatus</em> red ecotype than in the <em>D. dentatus</em> white ecotype (<a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f007" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>; <a href="#app1-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link">Supplemental Figures S2 and S5</a>). The secondary metabolites putatively identified, such as alkaloids and terpenoids, were comparable between the ecotypes (<a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>; <a href="#app1-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link">Supplemental Figures S3 and S4</a>).</p></section><section id="sec3dot5-metabolites-13-01050"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.5. Metabolite Annotation and Compound Class Contribution Using Tandem Mass Spectrometry Data</h3>
<p>We annotated 847 compounds ranging from levels 2 to 4 by combining the usage of GNPS, MS DIAL, and SIRIUS, and compounds without fragmentation data (MS<sup>1</sup>). Approximately 12% of compounds annotated did not have fragmentation data and were placed at annotation level 4. In pooling contributions of red and white ecotypes, with ClassyFire used to categorize the compounds, most of the superclass of compounds fell into benzenoids, lipids, and lipid-like molecules, and phenylpropanoids and polyketides at 13.7, 37.9, and 20%, respectively (<a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>). The phenolic compounds screened for therapeutic potential were majorly distributed in these categories. Although alkaloids were a superclass of interest, they were the second lowest contributor to the annotated compound pool at 0.6%.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="metabolites-13-01050-f008"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 8.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10608729_metabolites-13-01050-g008.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/6cb5/10608729/c690c8e7561c/metabolites-13-01050-g008.jpg" loading="lazy" height="734" width="727" alt="Figure 8"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/metabolites-13-01050-f008/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Sunburst plot of the different class levels of the nodes annotated by ClassyFire by compounds from both positive and negative ionization modes from both red and white <em>D. dentatus</em> ecotypes. ClassyFire summarizes the compounds according to the kingdom level (K), superclass level (SC), and class level (C). Benzenoid, phenylpropanoid, and polyketide, and lipids and lipid-like molecule superclasses dominate in contributions of annotated compounds.</p></figcaption></figure><p>In investigating phenylpropanoids and polyketides, the flavonoid class dominated the pool at 63.9%, followed by macrolides and monologues at 5.9%, with equal contributions of cinnamic acids and derivatives, isoflavonoids, and linear 1,3-diarylpropanoids at 5.3% each (<a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f009" class="usa-link">Figure 9</a>). Under lipids and lipid-like molecules, the fatty acyl class contributed most to the pool at 45.5%, followed by prenol lipids at 38%, and steroids and steroid derivatives at 6.54%. Terpenoids, another category of interest for therapeutic potential, fell into prenol lipids.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="metabolites-13-01050-f009"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 9.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10608729_metabolites-13-01050-g009.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/6cb5/10608729/ce204dd565ec/metabolites-13-01050-g009.jpg" loading="lazy" height="833" width="712" alt="Figure 9"></a></p>
