<figcaption><p>Flavonoid biomarkers of <em>D. dentatus</em> red and white ecotypes’ normalized peak areas (mean ± SE) of the top metabolite features identified by PLS-DA. Within each metabolite, the intensity is dependent on the combination of ecotypes (ANOVA red ecotype × white ecotype (control interaction)): proanthocyanidin A2 <em>p</em> &lt; 0.04, epicatechin methyl gallate <em>p</em> &lt; 0.015, and catechin gallate <em>p</em> &lt; 0.015. “*” and “ns” represent statistical significance and not significant, respectively.</p></figcaption></figure><p>With respect to the biosynthetic pathways, using the mummichog algorithm under MetaboAnalyst 5.0 for the initial screening of MS<sup>1</sup> data, the pathway analysis results aligned with the annotated compounds, especially with high contributions of compounds belonging to both flavonoid and terpenoid biosynthesis pathways (<a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>A,B).</p></section></section><section id="sec4-metabolites-13-01050"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">4. Discussion</h2>
<p>The Orbitrap used in the present work had a high resolving power and excellent mass accuracy, precise mass measurements, and broad analyte coverage, providing greater confidence in identification, especially for complex matrices [<a href="#B88-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B88-metabolites-13-01050">88</a>,<a href="#B89-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B89-metabolites-13-01050">89</a>,<a href="#B90-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B90-metabolites-13-01050">90</a>,<a href="#B91-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B91-metabolites-13-01050">91</a>,<a href="#B92-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B92-metabolites-13-01050">92</a>]. Moreover, the full-scan mode allowed the recording of unlimited compounds, which made it feasible for the retrospective analysis of any potential compounds of interest. The Orbitrap HRMS can also reduce the method cost and development time by not having to purchase multiple standards or in performing manual method set-ups. To date, Orbitrap HRMS has been extensively used in mass spectrometry metabolomics base research for targeted and non-targeted analyses. However, LC-MS has some drawbacks, including high instrumentation costs, complex data analysis, time-consuming data processing, and challenges related to sample preparation and matrix effects. Therefore, the metabolite annotation was performed by spectral library matching as a first step. Several candidates of <em>D. dentatus</em> red and white putative metabolites were then shortlisted. Those that showed identical names from KnapSack [<a href="#B33-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33-metabolites-13-01050">33</a>] (Shinbo, Nakamura et al. 2006), KEGG [<a href="#B34-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B34-metabolites-13-01050">34</a>] (Kanehisa, Sato et al. 2019), the Plant Metabolic Network using the PlantCyc database (<a href="https://plantcyc.org" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://plantcyc.org</a>, accessed on 10 June 2023) [<a href="#B35-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B35-metabolites-13-01050">35</a>], MetaboQuest [<a href="#B36-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36-metabolites-13-01050">36</a>], SciFinder [<a href="#B37-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B37-metabolites-13-01050">37</a>] and PubChem [<a href="#B38-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-metabolites-13-01050">38</a>], Global Nature Production Social Molecular Networking (GNPS), SIRIUS, and MS Dial were used to query fragments for putative identifications [<a href="#B3-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3-metabolites-13-01050">3</a>,<a href="#B15-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-metabolites-13-01050">15</a>]. Then, peak confirmation was performed with Xcalibur 4.1 having a mass error of ±10 ppm to determine whether these metabolites were present in the methanol extracts of <em>D. dentatus</em> red and white ecotypes.</p>
<section id="sec4dot1-metabolites-13-01050"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.1. Untargeted Metabolomics Analysis Revealed Metabolites Present in Different Ecotypes of Doliocarpus Dentatus</h3>
<p>Secondary metabolites comprise organic chemicals that plants, fungi, and microbes synthesize. These compounds play diverse roles in ecological and adaptive processes. Plants provide several ecological functions, including herbivore defense, pollinator attraction, and the facilitation of environmental adaptations [<a href="#B93-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B93-metabolites-13-01050">93</a>,<a href="#B94-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B94-metabolites-13-01050">94</a>]. The secondary metabolites, including flavonoids, terpenoids, and alkaloids, possess significant value in traditional medicine, pharmaceuticals, perfumery, and flavoring.</p>
<p>The metabolites identified in the different <em>D. dentatus</em> ecotypes were diverse, with some reported to have therapeutic benefits due to their varied bioactivities. Using the LC-MS/MS analysis, we found polyphenolic, terpenoid, and alkaloid metabolite classes, with polyphenols (flavonoids) being the most statistically significant and relatively abundant between the red and white ecotypes.</p>
<p>There was a statistically significant difference between the polyphenols in the <em>D. dentatus</em> red ecotype and the <em>D. dentatus</em> white ecotype (<a href="#metabolites-13-01050-f009" class="usa-link">Figure 9</a>). Flavonoids, such as epicatechin methyl gallate, catechin gallate, and proanthocyanidin A2, significantly differed in the <em>D. dentatus</em> red ecotype compared to the <em>D. dentatus</em> white ecotype based on the integrated peak areas. Epicatechin methyl gallate, catechin gallate, proanthocyanidin A2 and proanthocyanidin B2 isomers, quercetin 3 glycoside, apigenin, (+)-Catechin, leucocyanidin, anthocyanidin 3-O-beta-D-sambubioside, and resveratrol are different types of bioactive compounds commonly found in <em>D. dentatus</em> (see <a href="#metabolites-13-01050-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a> and <a href="#metabolites-13-01050-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>, <a href="#app1-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link">Supplemental Tables S1–S7</a>). These compounds belong to other classes of natural products and possess diverse biological activities. Epicatechin methyl gallate, a bioactive compound found in various plant sources, exhibits antioxidant and anti-inflammatory properties that have attracted significant scientific interest due to its potential health-promoting properties [<a href="#B95-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B95-metabolites-13-01050">95</a>]. One of its notable benefits is its ability to reduce oxidative stress and inflammation in cells.</p>
<section id="sec4dot1dot1-metabolites-13-01050"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">4.1.1. Flavonoids</h4>
<p>Flavonoids are a class of secondary metabolites mainly composed of a benzopyrone ring that contains phenolic or polyphenolic groups at various locations [<a href="#B96-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B96-metabolites-13-01050">96</a>] The biosynthesis of phenols relies on two pathways: the shikimic acid and malonic acid pathways [<a href="#B97-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B97-metabolites-13-01050">97</a>,<a href="#B98-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B98-metabolites-13-01050">98</a>]. These compounds are predominantly present in various botanical sources, such as fruits, herbs, stems, grains, nuts, vegetables, flowers, and seeds [<a href="#B99-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B99-metabolites-13-01050">99</a>,<a href="#B100-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B100-metabolites-13-01050">100</a>]. The therapeutic efficacy and biological activity of these various plant components are attributed to the presence of bioactive phytochemical substances [<a href="#B101-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B101-metabolites-13-01050">101</a>]. Flavonoids may offer antioxidant, anti-inflammatory, anticancer, cardiovascular, and neuroprotective benefits [<a href="#B102-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B102-metabolites-13-01050">102</a>], which may contribute to reducing several sexual dysfunctions, such as low sexual desire, erectile dysfunction, an inability to achieve orgasm, and premature ejaculation, which are relatively common in men, even at a young age [<a href="#B103-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B103-metabolites-13-01050">103</a>,<a href="#B104-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B104-metabolites-13-01050">104</a>,<a href="#B105-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B105-metabolites-13-01050">105</a>,<a href="#B106-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B106-metabolites-13-01050">106</a>,<a href="#B107-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B107-metabolites-13-01050">107</a>,<a href="#B108-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B108-metabolites-13-01050">108</a>].</p>
<p>Moreover, the research indicates that incorporating better lifestyle behaviors might enhance erectile function. Specifically, implementing dietary modifications and the augmentation of nutritional antioxidant consumption appear to be the most encouraging and economically viable strategies for addressing erectile dysfunction [<a href="#B109-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B109-metabolites-13-01050">109</a>,<a href="#B110-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B110-metabolites-13-01050">110</a>]. The consumption of several antioxidant-rich foods, such as pomegranate juice, coffee, wine, nuts, and ginseng, has been associated with lower ED prevalence [<a href="#B111-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B111-metabolites-13-01050">111</a>,<a href="#B112-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B112-metabolites-13-01050">112</a>,<a href="#B113-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B113-metabolites-13-01050">113</a>,<a href="#B114-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B114-metabolites-13-01050">114</a>,<a href="#B115-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B115-metabolites-13-01050">115</a>,<a href="#B116-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B116-metabolites-13-01050">116</a>].</p>
<p>In general, it has been proposed that incorporating a healthy diet, which emphasizes consuming fruits and vegetables while limiting animal protein intake, can potentially improve symptoms of ED. This dietary approach reduces glucose and lipid metabolism, enhances antioxidant defenses, and elevates arginine levels. Consequently, these effects contribute to increased nitric oxide (NO) activity, ultimately improving erectile function [<a href="#B116-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B116-metabolites-13-01050">116</a>,<a href="#B117-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B117-metabolites-13-01050">117</a>,<a href="#B118-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B118-metabolites-13-01050">118</a>,<a href="#B119-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B119-metabolites-13-01050">119</a>].</p>
<p>The justification for employing antioxidants to mitigate the symptoms of erectile dysfunction is derived from the discovery that ED frequently occurs before the manifestation of cardiovascular (CV) conditions. Antioxidant chemicals have gained recognition for their advantageous impact on mitigating cardiovascular risk, implying their potential to resolve symptoms of erectile dysfunction [<a href="#B120-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B120-metabolites-13-01050">120</a>].</p>
<p>Catechin gallate, found in <em>D.dentatus</em>, possesses intriguing properties that have attracted scientific attention (see <a href="#metabolites-13-01050-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a> and <a href="#metabolites-13-01050-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>). Similar to other catechins, it exhibits antioxidant activity and potential health benefits. Its antioxidant properties help reduce oxidative stress and prevent chronic diseases associated with free radicals, such as cardiovascular disease [<a href="#B121-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B121-metabolites-13-01050">121</a>,<a href="#B122-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B122-metabolites-13-01050">122</a>]. Catechin gallate also exhibits anti-inflammatory effects, mitigating inflammation and potentially reducing the risk of chronic inflammatory diseases. Additionally, it may positively impact cardiovascular health by improving lipid profiles, lowering blood pressure, and enhancing vascular function [<a href="#B123-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B123-metabolites-13-01050">123</a>,<a href="#B124-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B124-metabolites-13-01050">124</a>,<a href="#B125-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B125-metabolites-13-01050">125</a>]. It has antioxidant properties, which help protect cells from damage caused by harmful bodily molecules. (+)-Catechin may have several health benefits, including promoting cardiovascular health, reducing inflammation, and potentially aiding in weight management [<a href="#B126-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B126-metabolites-13-01050">126</a>,<a href="#B127-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B127-metabolites-13-01050">127</a>,<a href="#B128-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B128-metabolites-13-01050">128</a>].</p>
<p>Proanthocyanidin B2 is a specific type of proanthocyanidin, which is a type of flavonoid found in various plants. Proanthocyanidins are known for their antioxidant properties and are commonly found in foods, such as fruits, vegetables, nuts, and seeds, and certain beverages, such as red wine and tea [<a href="#B129-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B129-metabolites-13-01050">129</a>]. Proanthocyanidin B2 has been studied for its potential health benefits. It exhibits antioxidant activity, helping to neutralize harmful free radicals in the body [<a href="#B130-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B130-metabolites-13-01050">130</a>]. It also has anti-inflammatory properties, which may be beneficial in reducing inflammation-related conditions. Proanthocyanidin B2 may contribute to cardiovascular health by promoting healthy blood vessel function and reducing the risk of blood clot formation.</p>
<p>Additionally, it has been investigated for its potential anticancer properties, as it may help inhibit the growth of cancer cells [<a href="#B131-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B131-metabolites-13-01050">131</a>]. Proanthocyanidin A2 is a flavonoid subclass found in plants recognized for their antioxidant capabilities. It is generated by polymerizing flavan-3-ol monomers and is distinguished by its condensed tannin structure [<a href="#B129-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B129-metabolites-13-01050">129</a>]. Proanthocyanidins, especially Proanthocyanidin A2, have been linked to various health benefits, including antioxidant activity, cardiovascular health, anti-inflammatory properties, skin health, and dental health [<a href="#B132-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B132-metabolites-13-01050">132</a>].</p>
<p>Proanthocyanidins can neutralize dangerous free radicals, promote healthy blood flow, lower blood pressure, and enhance lipid profiles [<a href="#B133-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B133-metabolites-13-01050">133</a>]. Quercetin 3-glucoside, also known as isoquercitrin, is a natural plant compound found in fruits, vegetables, and herbs (see <a href="#metabolites-13-01050-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a> and <a href="#metabolites-13-01050-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>). It belongs to the glycoside class and has antioxidant properties that protect cells from free radical damage. Quercetin 3-glucoside also has anti-inflammatory effects, which may help reduce inflammation and manage inflammation-related conditions [<a href="#B134-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B134-metabolites-13-01050">134</a>,<a href="#B135-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B135-metabolites-13-01050">135</a>]. It has been studied for its potential cardiovascular benefits, such as improving blood vessel function, lowering blood pressure, and inhibiting blood clot formation. Quercetin 3-glucoside also modulates the immune system, potentially supporting immune function and defense against infections [<a href="#B136-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B136-metabolites-13-01050">136</a>]. Its antioxidant and anti-inflammatory properties may promote healthy skin by protecting against oxidative stress and inflammation [<a href="#B137-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B137-metabolites-13-01050">137</a>,<a href="#B138-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B138-metabolites-13-01050">138</a>].</p>
<p>Apigenin is a natural flavonoid molecule in many plants with antioxidant, anti-inflammatory, and anticancer activities [<a href="#B139-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B139-metabolites-13-01050">139</a>]. Its prevalence in plant-based meals, such as parsley, celery, and chamomile tea, contributes to its potential health advantages [<a href="#B140-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B140-metabolites-13-01050">140</a>]. Apigenin is an antioxidant that neutralizes damaging free radicals and lowers oxidative stress associated with chronic illnesses, such as cancer, cardiovascular disease, and neurological disorders [<a href="#B139-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B139-metabolites-13-01050">139</a>,<a href="#B141-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B141-metabolites-13-01050">141</a>]. It also possesses anti-inflammatory qualities, which aid in reducing inflammation and discomfort. Apigenin has been found in studies to cause cell cycle arrest and death in cancer cells, potentially reducing their growth and spread [<a href="#B141-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B141-metabolites-13-01050">141</a>,<a href="#B142-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B142-metabolites-13-01050">142</a>]. Apigenin has been studied for its potential for cancer prevention and therapy, including applications in breast, colon, prostate, and lung malignancies. It has also been examined for its potential to improve cardiovascular health, cognitive function, and immune system performance [<a href="#B143-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B143-metabolites-13-01050">143</a>,<a href="#B144-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B144-metabolites-13-01050">144</a>]. Leucocyanidin is a bioactive compound in plants belonging to the flavan-3-ol flavonoid family. It has potent antioxidant capabilities that protect cells from oxidative damage [<a href="#B145-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B145-metabolites-13-01050">145</a>]. Moreover, leucocyanidin has anti-inflammatory properties that may help minimize the risk of chronic inflammatory illnesses. It also benefits cardiovascular health by promoting healthy blood vessels and heart function [<a href="#B146-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B146-metabolites-13-01050">146</a>]. While further study is needed, leucocyanidin’s antioxidant, anti-inflammatory, and cardiovascular effects make it a promising molecule for overall well-being and disease prevention.</p>
<p>Anthocyanidin 3-O-beta-D-sambubioside is a unique anthocyanin pigment that contributes to the vibrant colors of fruits, vegetables, and flowers. Its attachment to beta-D-sambubioside makes it unique, contributing to the vibrant colors in plant-based foods and attracting pollinators for plant reproduction. Anthocyanidin 3-O-beta-D-sambubioside offers potential health benefits due to its antioxidant properties, which neutralize harmful free radicals in the body [<a href="#B147-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B147-metabolites-13-01050">147</a>,<a href="#B148-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B148-metabolites-13-01050">148</a>,<a href="#B149-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B149-metabolites-13-01050">149</a>,<a href="#B150-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B150-metabolites-13-01050">150</a>]. These free radicals can cause cellular damage and contribute to chronic diseases, such as cardiovascular disease and cancer [<a href="#B148-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B148-metabolites-13-01050">148</a>,<a href="#B151-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B151-metabolites-13-01050">151</a>]. Additionally, the research suggests that anthocyanidin 3-O-beta-D-sambubioside may have anti-inflammatory effects, modulating inflammatory responses and reducing the risk of chronic diseases [<a href="#B147-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B147-metabolites-13-01050">147</a>,<a href="#B152-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B152-metabolites-13-01050">152</a>]. The presence of anthocyanidin 3-O-beta-D-sambubioside in fruits and vegetables makes them visually appealing and potentially beneficial for health.</p>
<p>The bioactive compounds discussed previously, such as epicatechin methyl gallate, catechin gallate, (+)-Catechin, proanthocyanidins, quercetin 3-glucoside, and apigenin, offer various health benefits, especially for ED due to their antioxidant and anti-inflammatory properties [<a href="#B153-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B153-metabolites-13-01050">153</a>]. These compounds act as antioxidants, neutralizing harmful free radicals and reducing oxidative stress in cells, which helps protect against cellular damage and supports overall well-being. Additionally, they show potential in promoting cardiovascular health by improving lipid profiles, lowering blood pressure, and enhancing blood vessel function, which may reduce the risk of ED.</p></section><section id="sec4dot1dot2-metabolites-13-01050"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">4.1.2. Alkaloids</h4>
<p>Alkaloids are a class of organic molecules characterized by nitrogen atoms with elemental properties, often found in many organisms, including plants, fungi, and mammals [<a href="#B154-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B154-metabolites-13-01050">154</a>,<a href="#B155-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B155-metabolites-13-01050">155</a>]. The biosynthesis of alkaloids relies on the Shikimic acid and Malonic acid pathway [<a href="#B156-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B156-metabolites-13-01050">156</a>,<a href="#B157-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B157-metabolites-13-01050">157</a>]. These important secondary metabolites have been recognized for their medicinal potential [<a href="#B158-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B158-metabolites-13-01050">158</a>]. These compounds are categorized into several groups based on their biosynthetic precursor and heterocyclic ring system. These groups include indole, piperidine, tropane, purine, pyrrolizidine, imidazole, quinolozidine, isoquinoline, and alkaloids [<a href="#B159-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B159-metabolites-13-01050">159</a>]. Alkaloids may hinder the initiation of diverse degenerative ailments through their ability to scavenge free radicals or bind with catalysts involved in oxidative reactions [<a href="#B160-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B160-metabolites-13-01050">160</a>].</p>
<p>The secondary metabolites, such as trigonelline, xanthurenic acid, thiazole, and raphanin, are all present in <em>D. dentatus,</em> of which their relative abundance is higher in the <em>D. dentatus</em> white ecotype. At the same time, 4-Methylelletierine, N–Methylelletierine, Nicotinamide, Stachydrine, and Acetylbrowniine were higher in the <em>D. dentatus</em> red ecotype (see <a href="#app1-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link">Supplemental Tables S1–S7</a>).</p>
<p>Trigonelline exhibits antioxidant and anti-inflammatory properties, and may aid blood sugar regulation and metabolic health [<a href="#B161-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B161-metabolites-13-01050">161</a>]. Xanthurenic acid is involved in amino acid metabolism and is linked to regulating insulin levels and glucose metabolism [<a href="#B162-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B162-metabolites-13-01050">162</a>]. Thiazole shows diverse biological activities and has garnered interest in medicinal chemistry [<a href="#B163-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B163-metabolites-13-01050">163</a>]. Methylelletierine and N-Methylelletierine possess anthelmintic properties, which are beneficial for combating parasitic infections. Nicotinamide and stachydrine are associated with anti-hypertensive effects and affect blood pressure regulation [<a href="#B164-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B164-metabolites-13-01050">164</a>]. Acetylbrowniine offers anti-inflammatory properties [<a href="#B165-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B165-metabolites-13-01050">165</a>], while rapanin, derived from radishes, has potential antioxidant and anticancer effects [<a href="#B166-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B166-metabolites-13-01050">166</a>]. These compounds interact with biological systems in distinct ways, offering a range of potential health benefits; however, further research is needed for a comprehensive understanding of their effects.</p>
<p>Secondary metabolites 4-Methylelletrine, N-Methylelletrine, Nicotinamide, Stachydrine, Trigonelline, and Acetylbrowniine have not been subjected to comprehensive investigations as potential therapeutic interventions for erectile dysfunction. Although several compounds may possess features that might potentially impact the aspects associated with ED, such as regulating blood pressure, cardiovascular well-being, and blood sugar control, there is currently insufficient data to substantiate their efficacy as primary therapies for ED.</p></section><section id="sec4dot1dot3-metabolites-13-01050"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">4.1.3. Terpenoids</h4>
<p>Terpenoids are a large and diverse class of structurally secondary metabolites derived from five-carbon isoprene units naturally occurring in many plants [<a href="#B167-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B167-metabolites-13-01050">167</a>]. The biosynthesis of the mevalonic acid pathway relies on the shikimic acid and malonic acid pathway [<a href="#B156-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B156-metabolites-13-01050">156</a>,<a href="#B157-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B157-metabolites-13-01050">157</a>,<a href="#B168-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B168-metabolites-13-01050">168</a>]. Terpenoids differ due to their functional groups and C10-unit hydrocarbon backbone. These are further categorized based on the number of carbocyclic rings, such as monoterpenes (limonene, carvone, (1R)-(-)-Nopol and carveol), diterpenes (the retinoids), and tetraterpenes (α- and β-carotene, lutein, lycopene, zeaxanthine, betulinic acid, betulin, and cryptoxanthine) [<a href="#B169-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B169-metabolites-13-01050">169</a>,<a href="#B170-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B170-metabolites-13-01050">170</a>]. These subclasses may have anti-inflammatory, anti-cancer, antioxidant, anti-inflammatory, and antimicrobial properties [<a href="#B171-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B171-metabolites-13-01050">171</a>]. Terpenoids are also essential for plant growth and development, impart flavor, aroma, and color to plants’ foliage, flowers, and fruits [<a href="#B172-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B172-metabolites-13-01050">172</a>,<a href="#B173-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B173-metabolites-13-01050">173</a>], and defend them against insects, herbivores, and fungal diseases [<a href="#B174-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B174-metabolites-13-01050">174</a>].</p>
<p>Secondary metabolites, such as alpha-pinene, ascaridole, (1R)-(-)-Nopol, 3-(beta-D-glucopyranosyloxy)-2-methyl-4H-Pyran-4-one, 4-(2,6,6-trimethyl-2-cyclohexen-1-yl)- 2-Butanone, farnesol, betulinic acid, betulonic acid, sumaresinolic acid, betulin, and saikogenin D, are all present in <em>D. dentatus,</em> of which their relative abundance is comparable between <em>D. dentatus</em> red and white ecotypes (see <a href="#metabolites-13-01050-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a> and <a href="#metabolites-13-01050-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a> and <a href="#app1-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link">Supplemental Tables S1–S7</a>).</p>
<p>These compounds have diverse bioactive characteristics, from their potential use in medicine to their roles as natural antibacterial agents, antioxidants, and enhancers of olfactory and gustatory sensations. Nevertheless, it is imperative to highlight that the precise bioactivities and possible health advantages of these compounds are currently being investigated, and their efficacy may differ based on the particular circumstances in which they are utilized, thus requiring additional investigations.</p>
<p>The current body of research on the influence of terpenoids on ED is still constrained. However, it is important to highlight that terpenoids, naturally present in various plants, can indirectly impact aspects related to ED. Terpenoids with anti-inflammatory and antioxidant characteristics have promising potential in enhancing vascular health and mitigating oxidative stress, pertinent factors in ED. Specific terpenoids have the potential to facilitate vasodilation, which can enhance blood circulation and thus have an indirect impact on improving erectile function [<a href="#B175-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B175-metabolites-13-01050">175</a>]. Moreover, several terpenoids, recognized for their anxiolytic and stress-alleviating properties, can alleviate psychological elements contributing to ED. Additional investigations are necessary to clarify the exact processes and effectiveness of terpenoids in managing ED, considering their significant potential in treating this complex ailment.</p></section><section id="sec4dot1dot4-metabolites-13-01050"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">4.1.4. Therapeutic Compounds and Possible Synergistic Effects</h4>
<p>The compounds discussed in this research demonstrated many synergistic interactions, particularly in the domains of fragrance, possible therapeutic effects, and anti-cancer capabilities [<a href="#B176-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B176-metabolites-13-01050">176</a>]. The combination of alpha-pinene and (1R)-(-)-Nopol in essential oils creates pleasant scents that contribute to the overall olfactory attractiveness of these oils. The potential exists for a synergistic interaction between farnesol and saikikogenin D, resulting in enhanced anti-inflammatory and antibacterial activities, hence increasing their therapeutic efficacy [<a href="#B177-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B177-metabolites-13-01050">177</a>]. In the context of anticancer activities, it has been shown that compounds, such as betulin, betulonic acid, betulinic acid, and sumaresinolic acid, may potentially collaborate to produce favorable outcomes. The degree of synergy between 3-(beta-D-glucopyranosyloxy)-2-methyl-4H-Pyran-4-one and 4-(2,6,6-trimethyl-2-cyclohexen-1-yl)-2-Butanone is contingent upon the specific applications in which they are utilized [<a href="#B178-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B178-metabolites-13-01050">178</a>].</p>
<p>In the domain of ED, secondary metabolites, such as flavonoids, alkaloids, terpenoids, and other phytochemicals in whole-plant extracts have been observed to facilitate favorable interactions, presenting potential advantages [<a href="#B179-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B179-metabolites-13-01050">179</a>]. These interactions may augment blood circulation, mitigate inflammation, and increase vascular and sexual well-being. The research shows that green tea polyphenols, particularly epigallocatechin gallate (EGCG), have antioxidant properties that may reduce oxidative stress in the body, which is known to contribute to blood vessel damage and impaired blood flow, factors often linked to ED [<a href="#B95-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B95-metabolites-13-01050">95</a>]. Yohimbine is an alkaloid found in yohimbe bark, and it is known to have vasodilatory effects, which means it can relax and widen blood vessels, improving blood circulation. In the context of ED, an increased blood flow to the penis is essential for achieving and maintaining an erection [<a href="#B180-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B180-metabolites-13-01050">180</a>]. Ginseng contains various terpenoids that may enhance nitric oxide production. Nitric oxide is a signaling molecule that plays a crucial role in dilating blood vessels and promoting blood flow to the penis, which may improve erectile function [<a href="#B116-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B116-metabolites-13-01050">116</a>,<a href="#B117-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B117-metabolites-13-01050">117</a>,<a href="#B118-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B118-metabolites-13-01050">118</a>,<a href="#B119-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B119-metabolites-13-01050">119</a>].</p>
<p>The synergistic effects of polyphenol-rich extracts derived from sources, such as green tea, dark chocolate, and berries; alkaloids present in yohimbine and ginseng; and terpenoids sourced from herbs, such as ginkgo biloba and ginseng, may potentially collaborate to enhance blood circulation, induce vasodilation, and augment nitric oxide synthesis, thereby potentially contributing to the amelioration of erectile dysfunction [<a href="#B181-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B181-metabolites-13-01050">181</a>,<a href="#B182-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B182-metabolites-13-01050">182</a>,<a href="#B183-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B183-metabolites-13-01050">183</a>].</p>
<p>Moreover, the aforementioned phytochemical interactions have the potential to offer antioxidant assistance, hence mitigating the detrimental effects of oxidative stress on blood vessels and the consequential impairment of erectile function. The potential enhancement of sexual health and the alleviation of symptoms related to ED can be achieved through the synergistic actions of polyphenols, alkaloids, and terpenoids derived from various sources.</p>
<p>Reactive Oxygen Species (ROS) play important roles in various diseases, including cancer, obesity, and ED [<a href="#B184-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B184-metabolites-13-01050">184</a>,<a href="#B185-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B185-metabolites-13-01050">185</a>,<a href="#B186-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B186-metabolites-13-01050">186</a>,<a href="#B187-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B187-metabolites-13-01050">187</a>]. ROS can negatively impact the function of blood vessels in the penis, leading to vascular dysfunction. Excessive ROS levels can cause oxidative damage to the endothelial cells that line the blood vessels, reducing their ability to produce and release nitric oxide (NO). NO plays a crucial role in vasodilation, essential for achieving and maintaining an erection [<a href="#B187-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B187-metabolites-13-01050">187</a>]. ROS-induced endothelial dysfunction reduces NO bioavailability, resulting in inadequate blood flow to the penile tissues and erectile dysfunction [<a href="#B188-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B188-metabolites-13-01050">188</a>,<a href="#B189-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B189-metabolites-13-01050">189</a>].</p>
<p>Moreover, ROS can directly interact with and deactivate NO, further exacerbating the impairment of NO signaling in erectile function [<a href="#B190-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B190-metabolites-13-01050">190</a>]. NO is a vital signaling molecule that induces smooth muscle relaxation in the penile tissue, facilitating the dilation of blood vessels and the influx of blood necessary for an erection [<a href="#B188-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B188-metabolites-13-01050">188</a>]. Increased ROS production can lead to the formation of reactive nitrogen species (RNS), which react with NO to create peroxynitrite, a highly reactive compound that impairs NO-mediated vasodilation [<a href="#B191-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B191-metabolites-13-01050">191</a>].</p>
<p>The penile tissue is susceptible to oxidative damage caused by ROS. Excessive ROS levels induce oxidative stress, leading to lipid peroxidation, protein oxidation, and DNA damage within penile cells. These harmful effects disrupt the normal cellular function and integrity of the penile tissue, impairing its ability to achieve and sustain an erection [<a href="#B192-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B192-metabolites-13-01050">192</a>]. Furthermore, ROS-induced oxidative stress can trigger inflammatory responses in the penile tissue. Chronic inflammation can activate fibroblasts and promote the deposition of extracellular matrix proteins, leading to fibrosis [<a href="#B193-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B193-metabolites-13-01050">193</a>]. Penile fibrosis involves an excessive accumulation of collagen and other fibrous components, resulting in structural changes and the stiffness of the erectile tissue, further contributing to erectile dysfunction [<a href="#B194-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B194-metabolites-13-01050">194</a>].</p>
<p>The detrimental effects of ROS-induced damage on erectile function are multifaceted. Excessive ROS production can impair vascular function, disrupt NO signaling, cause tissue oxidative damage, promote inflammation and fibrosis, and contribute to age-related erectile dysfunction. Understanding the mechanisms through which ROS influences erectile function can guide the development of therapeutic strategies to mitigate oxidative stress and preserve erectile health. However, further research is needed to understand their effects and specific applications fully.</p>
<p>Plants exhibit an impressive capacity for synthesizing a wide array of organic compounds. Upon conducting an LC-MS/MS analysis on Guyana Capadulla ecotypes, we identified a diverse range of secondary metabolites, such as alkaloids, terpenoids, fatty acids, phenylpropanoids, organic acid, polyketides and gallotannins, lignans, and flavonoids. These organic compounds are believed to have evolved as a part of plant defense mechanisms against various environmental challenges, including pests, diseases, and droughts [<a href="#B195-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B195-metabolites-13-01050">195</a>]. Notably, among the identified metabolites that were found in both ecotypes in more than one sample were, salsolinol (alkaloid), lutein (carotenoids), and lyoniside (lignans: iridoid glycosides) <a href="#app1-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link">(Supplemental Tables S4–S6)</a>. Salsolinol, found in Guyanese Capadulla, is a natural compound in the isoquinoline alkaloid class that is a combination of dopamine and acetaldehyde [<a href="#B196-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B196-metabolites-13-01050">196</a>]. The research focuses on its effects on addiction and reward pathways in the brain, particularly its role in reinforcing drug-seeking behavior and addiction to substances, such as alcohol, nicotine, and opioids [<a href="#B197-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B197-metabolites-13-01050">197</a>]. Salsolinol’s interactions with neurotransmitter systems in the brain may contribute to the pleasurable effects of drugs and the development of addictive behaviors. Additionally, it has been studied for its potential neurotoxic effects, leading to oxidative stress and damage to neurons, which can be relevant to neurodegenerative diseases [<a href="#B196-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B196-metabolites-13-01050">196</a>]. However, further research is needed to fully understand its mechanisms and potential therapeutic applications.</p>
<p>In particular, green leafy vegetables, such as spinach, kale, and broccoli, contain lutein, a naturally occurring carotenoid pigment [<a href="#B198-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B198-metabolites-13-01050">198</a>]. It is an effective antioxidant that protects cells from the damage caused by risky free radicals, which can result in chronic diseases and aging [<a href="#B199-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B199-metabolites-13-01050">199</a>]. Because it is a vital component of the macular pigment, which serves as the macula’s protective layer, lutein is also crucial for maintaining eye health. This pigment protects retinal tissues from ultraviolet- and high-energy blue-light damage, reducing the risk of age-related macular degeneration (AMD) and cataracts. A diet high in lutein may be linked to a better cognitive performance and a lower risk of cognitive decline in older people, according to the research on the possible effects of lutein on cognitive function [<a href="#B200-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B200-metabolites-13-01050">200</a>]. To conclusively link lutein intake and cognitive health, additional research is necessary. A diet high in fruits and vegetables, primarily one high in lutein, can support general health and the maintenance of healthy eyes.</p>
<p>Specific plant sources contain a naturally occurring flavonoid glycoside called lyoniside, which has potent anti-inflammatory and antioxidant properties [<a href="#B201-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B201-metabolites-13-01050">201</a>], and is putatively identified in Guyanese Capadulla. It functions as an antioxidant to protect cells from the damage of harmful free radicals, which can accelerate aging and lead to chronic diseases. Furthermore, lyoniside has demonstrated its potential as a neuroprotective compound, protecting neurons and promoting brain health [<a href="#B202-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B202-metabolites-13-01050">202</a>]. Although lyoniside research is still in its early stages, the novel findings indicate that it has a high potential for therapeutic use, particularly in treating inflammatory and oxidative stress-related diseases.</p></section></section><section id="sec4dot2-metabolites-13-01050"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.2. Several Metabolites Are Ecotype Specific</h3>
<p>We described ecotype-specific metabolites as those present in only one ecotype <a href="#app1-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link">(Supplemental Tables S2 and S3)</a>.</p>
<p>For instance, (-)-Epigallocatechin, anthocyanidin 3-O-beta-D-sambubioside, and naringenin <a href="#app1-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link">(Supplemental Tables S2)</a>, which were present only in the <em>D. dentatus</em> red ecotype, are three bioactive compounds with significant potential in various fields. Epigallocatechin, found in green tea, exhibits antioxidant and anti-inflammatory properties, contributing to its health benefits [<a href="#B203-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B203-metabolites-13-01050">203</a>]. Anthocyanidin 3-O-beta-D-sambubioside, a plant flavonoid, presents antioxidant and anticancer properties, potentially aiding disease prevention [<a href="#B204-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B204-metabolites-13-01050">204</a>]. Naringenin, commonly found in citrus fruits, has anti-inflammatory and cardiovascular health-promoting effects, making it valuable for overall well-being [<a href="#B205-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B205-metabolites-13-01050">205</a>,<a href="#B206-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B206-metabolites-13-01050">206</a>]. These compounds hold promise for further research and applications in medicine, nutrition, and functional foods.</p>
<p>Quercetin and myricetin, present only in the <em>D. dentatus</em> white ecotype, are two flavonoids renowned for their high bioactive characteristics and multiple health benefits [<a href="#B207-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B207-metabolites-13-01050">207</a>]. Quercetin is present in various fruits and vegetables, including apples, onions, and berries, and is known for its antioxidant, anti-inflammatory, and immune-modulating properties. It has been examined for its ability to reduce the risk of chronic illnesses and improve heart health [<a href="#B208-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B208-metabolites-13-01050">208</a>]. For instance, a comprehensive study revealed that quercetin displayed remarkable antioxidant activity in human blood plasma, effectively neutralizing harmful free radicals. Furthermore, a study conducted on rats demonstrated that quercetin had anti-inflammatory properties by inhibiting the production of pro-inflammatory molecules, such as tumor necrosis factor-alpha (TNF-α) and interleukin-6 (IL-6), leading to a reduction in the symptoms associated with inflammatory diseases [<a href="#B209-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B209-metabolites-13-01050">209</a>,<a href="#B210-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B210-metabolites-13-01050">210</a>]. These scientific findings provide compelling evidence supporting the potential health benefits of quercetin and its significance as a valuable dietary component.</p>
<p>Myricetin, on the other hand, is found in foods, such as berries, grapes, and tea, and has comparable antioxidant and anti-inflammatory qualities. Myricetin may have neuroprotective and anticancer properties, according to the research. These adaptable flavonoids are gaining attention in scientific studies as vital components of a balanced diet and possible medicinal possibilities [<a href="#B208-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B208-metabolites-13-01050">208</a>]. For instance, the research has revealed that a diet abundant in berries, grapes, and tea, rich sources of myricetin can lower the risk of neurodegenerative diseases, such as Alzheimer’s disease [<a href="#B209-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B209-metabolites-13-01050">209</a>]. Moreover, studies have indicated that myricetin can impede the growth of cancer cells and trigger apoptosis, suggesting its potential as a prospective candidate for future cancer treatments [<a href="#B210-metabolites-13-01050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B210-metabolites-13-01050">210</a>].</p>
<p>Vendors of natural products in Guyana (known as herbalists colloquially) can use this information on ecotype-specific metabolites; by leveraging the evidence-based research, herbalists can enhance their practices in selecting, preparing, dosing, and utilizing plant materials for medicinal purposes. Scientific insights aid in proper ecotype selection, ensuring authenticity and quality. Herbalists can adopt standardized preparation methods and understand the mechanisms of action of medicinal plants. Moreover, scientific knowledge guides the dosage recommendations, identifies potential interactions, and helps in monitoring treatment effectiveness. By sharing evidence-based information, herbalists can educate patients and contribute to the research, leading to safer and more effective herbal medicine practices.</p></section></section><section id="sec5-metabolites-13-01050"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">5. Conclusions</h2>
<p>The present study aimed to analyze the phytochemical profiles of <em>D. dentatus</em> ecotypes using untargeted and semi-targeted plant metabolomics, employing liquid chromatography in conjunction with electrospray ionization mass spectrometry. Our findings highlight the presence of the diverse bioactive compounds in <em>D. dentatus</em> ecotypes, including polyphenolic compounds, such as epicatechin methyl gallate, catechin gallate, proanthocyanidin A2 and proanthocyanidin B2 isomers, quercetin 3 glycoside, apigenin, (+)-Catechin, leucocyanidin, anthocyanidin 3-O-beta-D-sambubioside, and resveratrol. These compounds exhibited diverse beneficial biological activities. While this study primarily focused on metabolomics, our future investigations will include antioxidant assays. Antioxidant assays and analyses are integral in comprehending the intricate mechanisms that shield organisms from oxidative stress. These assays thoroughly examine compounds and substances, assessing their potential to counteract the harmful impacts of free radicals and reactive oxygen species. By conducting these assays, the researchers can amass the critical data regarding the efficacy of different antioxidants in mitigating oxidative stress. Using other solvents for metabolite extraction, isolating key therapeutic compounds of interest with confirmation using nuclear magnetic resonance, and testing for bioactivity on select cell lines are potential avenues for future research. This research may provide invaluable insights into the potential interventions and treatments for conditions linked to oxidative stress, encompassing cardiovascular disease, erectile dysfunction, and cancer.</p></section><section id="ack1" class="ack"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Acknowledgments</h2>

The authors recognize The Ontario Research Fund and the Canadian Foundation for Innovation provided funding to purchase mass spectrometers and other instrumentation in the Water Quality Centre at Trent University.</p></section><section id="app1-metabolites-13-01050" class="app"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Supplementary Materials</h2>
